# Experiment ledger

One row per experiment. Columns:
- id
- paper / claim
- question
- procedure (sample, edit, control, metric)
- result (numbers)
- what it tells us
- effect on the thesis (supports / weakens / refines / neutral)

Earlier results (J1–J33, G1–G206) are in `distill-part/jld-features/research/FINDINGS.md` and are cited by id.

| id | paper/claim | question | status | result | tells us | thesis |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | A1, B | how many units per layer are dead, and how many change ≥1% of calls when set to their mean | done | 10,000 test jets (2,000/class), acc 81.29 %. Never firing / changing ≥1 % of calls: l1 49/64 · 13; P 50 · 11; g 49 · 7 (11 at ≥0.1 %); s 55 · 9; u/S 45 · 19; h4 9 · 42; h5 0 · 30; z 0 · 14. Largest single costs: a32 64.2 %, s14 64.9 %, u29 61.2 %, z1 21.6 % | the particle stage is about 13 cuts, the first objects about 11, the second-round objects 19; the post-pooling MLP is dense | supports A1 (small program) |
| E2 | A1 | each l1 unit as a cut in GeV and rad | done | live cuts (corrected by E16: 4 pT ramps; a4 and a50 are constant on data): pT ramps (a32: 3.86 − 0.352·pT, on for pT < 11.0 GeV, cost 64 %; a56: pT < 31.0 GeV; a15: 0.088·pT + 3.94, always on) and 9 angular half-planes through ≤0.01 rad of the axis in ~8 directions (a29: Δη > 0.007; a36: Δφ < −0.0024; a6: 0.62Δη + 0.78Δφ > 0.008 …), with zero pT coefficient | particle statements are "how soft" ramps and "which side of the axis" cuts; the angular objects are particle-count weighted, not pT weighted | supports A1; new: angular objects not IRC safe |
| E3 | A1, B | does Σ_i ReLU(w·x_i+b) = b·count + w·Σ_A x hold on the pooled layer P | done | exact (max error 0.0) on the network's own rounded inputs before the pooling quantizer; the pooling quantizer rounds each particle's value per slot (unit 32, slot 63: values snap to 2 or 4), mean |q(a)−a| 0.17–0.25. Variance split of P: P15 = ΣpT (share 1.00); P32 = 0.56 count + 0.44 ΣpT of particles below 11 GeV; angular P units = one-sided Σ Δη / Σ Δφ over particles on one side (shares 0.85–1.03) | each first-round object is one cut read by its count and summed (pT, Δη, Δφ); fixed-point rounding turns soft-particle ramps toward counts | supports A1/B (exact in float) |
| E3q | A1, B | does the decision depend on the fixed-point rounding of the first layers | done | same 10,000 jets; quantizers skipped: l1 input 2.62 % calls changed (acc 81.21 %); qs 1.48 % (81.43); qs1 2.76 % (80.88); l1+qs+qs1 3.83 % (81.02); all quantizers 51.6 % (43.71) | the particle and object stages can be written in float at a 3.8 % call cost; a later quantizer (likely the last layer's modulo wrap) is part of the computation | refines A1: "exact up to 3.8 % of calls" |
| E3w | A1, B | which quantizer does the all-float network (43.7 %) lack | done | 10,000 jets; float everywhere except one layer's quantizer: except l3 input 78.25 % (8.0 % calls changed), except qs1 78.83 % (8.1 %), every other single layer 39–54 %; float last layer d7 alone 0.30 % calls changed (its modulo wrap is not used) | the second-round quantizers carry something the network needs | led to E3m |
| E3m | A1, A3, B | are HGQ's zero-bit entries (fixed per slot × channel masks) the missing part | done | zero-bit fractions: qs 81.6 %, l2 79.4 %, dd 78.1 %, l3 80.1 %, qs1 84.2 %, d4 70.3 %, d5 12.5 %. Float + masks only: 80.68 % (fixed 81.29 %), 4.49 % calls changed; fully float 43.71 %. Slot windows of live S objects: u0, u43 = 14 hardest particles; u19 = slots 13–63; u1 = all but the leading particle; u29, u30, all 64 | the program is float arithmetic plus fixed membership masks: each object = cut ∧ pT-rank window; rounding and wrap are a 4.5 % perturbation | refines A1/A3: objects are defined by a cut AND a rank window |
| E8 | A6 | are classes conjunctions of neuron windows on JEDI's own z and S, and does imposing the conjunction cause the class | done; verified (E8v, confidence 0.6) | 30,000 jets (seed 4), trees on 20,000, evaluation 10,000. z rules (3 windows each): precision 95.8–98.4 %, coverage 69.5–82.8 %. Rule units set to in-rule medians on jets called otherwise: g 54.4, q 31.2, W 42.0, Z 62.0, t 25.9 %; all 16 neurons 100 %; random draws without a rule unit 1.4–4.1 % (all draws 10.2–12.4 %). Necessity (rule units → medians of other calls): g 0.0 %, q 95.3, W 22.9, Z 57.9, t 91.0 % still called c. At S: g 47.8, q 20.5, W 5.2, Z 14.7, t 33.1 % (W random 6.9 %). E8v (fresh disjoint 30,000 jets, seed 23): precision 96.2–98.4 %, coverage 69.7–82.0 %, switched 54.0/31.8/40.6/61.4/26.2 %; rule's set ranks 6/39/10/6/78 of all 560 z triples with the same value source (share of triples switching at least as much 0.011/0.070/0.018/0.011/0.14); highest triples 61.6/52.5/48.3/75.7/77.3 % (t: {z8, z10, z13}); triples without a rule unit mean 3.8–7.7 %, max 12.8–58.1 %; moving rule units just inside the windows 7.2–25.1 % (edge 3.4–12.8 %); 3-unit logit change = sum of single-unit changes (max diff 0.0); rules in 5 random rotations of z: precision 87–99 %, coverage 46–82 %; refit changes one unit for q, W, Z | the window rules describe the calls of unedited jets with ≥ 96 % precision, but similar rules exist in rotated bases; the causal effect follows how far the units move along d7's additive logit margin, not window membership; z0, z7 are E6's W and Z windows, z8 (124–170 GeV) is not E6's top window z10; E8's random control was not value-matched and its untrained control is degenerate (calls every jet t) | refines A6 (description holds; "imposing the conjunction causes the class" holds only for the typical-value edit) |
| E14 | A3, A4 | how round two builds near-radial objects from the nine half-planes, and whether the per-particle maps carry the direction term of the mass read-out | done; verified (E14v, confidence 0.7) | 10,000 jets (seed 8); float + masks program 80.30 %. 15 particle-level u units take half-plane input through five s units (s43, s60, s53, s32, s52); on-contours max/min 1.14–1.47; Part-1 weight polygon rms ≤ 0.005 for 60/78 contours (max 0.064). Translation δ 0.03: odd/even of the h5 mass read-out maps −0.143/−0.092/−0.173 vs measured (g frozen) −0.144/−0.096/−0.174 (η/φ/diag); point-symmetrized or θ-averaged maps ≤ 0.0008. One half-plane → 0: 7.0–29.0 % calls changed (g frozen), 99 % of the change on the own half. E14v (seed 31): Part 1/2 reproduce within 4 points; 11 single-path units, u13, u43, u40, u60 combine 2–3 s units; nonzero half-planes per unit 3–9 (all nine only u40); best circle rms 0.032–0.086, random directions 0.046–0.083 (below the circle in 6/17), real directions 0–0.026; odd(maps)/odd(measured, g frozen) 0.96–1.05, 95 % CIs within [0.93, 1.08]; sample-asymmetry term B −0.0005 to +0.0003 (p ≥ 0.20, H0 B = 0 not rejected); symmetrized network odd +0.0000 [−0.0005, +0.0005]; context share of odd part 15–24 % (seed 8: 9–21 %) | round two forms each near-radial object as the level set of a weighted sum of 3–9 half-planes in one to three s units; the contours are polygons only 3–9 % from a circle, so random-direction polygons fit about as well; the direction term of the mass read-out is the sum of the per-particle maps and vanishes when they are symmetrized; E14's hybrid counterfactual sends negative S into the unsigned fixed-point head (sign flip) and is replaced by the symmetrized-network construction | refines A3 (construction measured; not a nine-direction polygon); supports A4's odd-term explanation |
| E15 | A4, A9 | do h4/h5 combine M0 = ΣpT and M2 = Σ pT r² as a product or as a sum | done; refuted as stated by E15v (confidence 0.2) | 10,000 jets (seed 11). S0 responds to M0 only (e_k 0.00), S50 to M2 only (e_c 0.05). Separable factorial A2 (pT × c, angles × k/√c): read-out β (sum 0, product 1) 0.33/0.70/0.60/0.32 at S/h4/h5/z; M0-only ÷ pT×c response at h4 0.629/0.449/0.477 by M2 third (product 0.5 flat, sum 0.75/0.50/0.30). E15v (fresh 9,639 jets, seed 23): β 0.42/0.70/0.53/0.32; same read-out procedure on known populations: sum held linearly 0.00, ReLU windows on the sum −0.73 to −0.77, product 1.00, ReLU on separate M0, M2 0.90–1.15, random untrained ReLU layer on the trained S −0.01/0.42/1.14; read-outs of V_sum / V_prod from h4 give 0.03 / 0.71; refitting the read-out moves β by up to 0.09 (bootstrap se 0.0005–0.0008 omits this). Gain-normalised M0 response: S 0.62/0.58/0.55, h4 0.63/0.44/0.47, z 0.65/0.54/0.53; random ReLU layers on S 0.49–0.66/0.44–0.48/0.46–0.50. Matched M0×c vs M2×c steps (product d = 0; sum +0.47/0/−0.48): h4 median d +0.61/+0.15/+0.02, IQR width ≈ 1.5; the M0 step also moves pT-threshold features (calls differ between the two steps for 33.6 % of jets) | the β statistic does not separate sum from product; S holds M0 and M2 in separate units and the read-out matches the product within 0.06 for M2 > 8.1 GeV from S onward, which random ReLU layers on S also do; where the product forms (h4/h5) is not located by this test | weakens A4's "multiplied in h4/h5" and A9's product step (open); supports "read separately at S" |
| E10 | A9 | are high-level variables built over the layers, in order of algebraic depth | done (not yet skeptic-rerun; statistics in R2) | 20,000 jets (seed 0), 45 observables with a depth label, ridge + MLP read-outs at P, g, S, h4, h5, z; untrained control. Spearman depth vs first stage at R² ≥ 0.9: ρ 0.51 (p 4e-4); at 0.7: 0.38 (p 0.009). Depth 0–1 reach 0.9 at P or S; depth 2–3 mostly never and peak at h4. Causal: multiplicity edit, probe at S slope 0.52 (rotation control −0.004); girth edit slope 0.91 (control mean |Δ| 0.0018; side effect mass +18 GeV); D2 at fixed mass slope 0.057, corr 0.28 (control 0.30). Untrained R² ≤ 0.006 at S, h4. R2: ρ 0.507 [0.27, 0.71], permutation p 5e-4 | readability rises with depth; D2 is readable in data but its probe does not follow a D2 edit; readability is not use | supports A9 (readability); weakens "use" for depth ≥ 2 |

Radial crossing edits: S29 +0.482 vs predicted +0.469 (control +0.008); S1 +0.457 vs +0.446; random-r slope 1.03–1.04 | 17 of 19 objects are two-variable hinges with a fixed rank window; S31, S58 are functions of g only | supports A3 (v2) |

(b) b2h8 mean ablation changes 0.85 % of W/Z calls (p 0.86), b1h3 12.1 %; G21b at full stats (angles × 1.1): Δz97 −0.345 = attention −0.118 (0.34) + value −0.236 + interaction 0.009. P3's "b1h3 19 %" was the diluted resample slope. Particle far-side heads exceed random only on W jets under real mass edits, mostly head 4. With particle attention, skip maps and embeddings computed on the real jet, ranked feature circuits reach 51 % W/Z at 50 features and 74–78 % at 1,000–2,000 (random 18–22 %); they are almost all class-level features; the live attention/skip paths alone give donor-level accuracy (17 %). With attention and skip maps also resampled, every variant stays at 42–43 %. The single m_A-correlated feature does not carry the φ response at calibrated steps or under ablation; at 4–8 s.d. it moves calls toward W. Joint steering reproduces the natural m_A read-out slope, which is about 1/7 of the φ edit's logit change. P13's single-feature steering criterion (≥ 0.6 of φ) could not be met under its own calibration. Conclusion: learned sparse features ranked by attribution did not give a small circuit for ParT_full's W-versus-Z decision; 50 features kept alone give about 51 % against the model's 83 %, and 75 % needs 1,000–2,000 features, only while attention and the transcoders' linear bypass are computed on the real jet. The axis-moment-mass information is not in one feature; moving all features of a class-token layer together reproduces how the score varies with m_A in natural jets, which is about a seventh of the φ-shift response; trained (not ranked) feature circuits are untested | weakens the P13 thesis (C8 reverses with learned features); supports C8 at the feature level (ranked selection); C7: single-feature coupling not supported |

An unfitted mean-difference direction and a freshly initialised network reach the same summed DAS share | corrected reading (P15v): ParticleNet's W/Z score responds equally to φ + 0.06 and φ − 0.06. The EdgeConv-1 direction fitted on + shifts records the sign of the shift and reproduces −0.2 to −0.3 of the − response; the EdgeConv-2 direction is sign-symmetric and reproduces 0.73–0.80 of each sign; one-direction matches of this size also appear in shuffled or freshly initialised networks, so they do not show a trained variable. An m_A copy is excluded at ec1–ec2 (ratios 0.11–0.39 vs 1). Freezing the rebuilt kNN graphs removes 0.7–4.0 % of the response. Pooled 32 (full) / 16 (kin) channels kept alone match W/Z accuracy on three samples but not 10-class accuracy (selection by W-vs-Z attribution). No basis-alignment statement holds (sign depends on the rotation control). EFP / Lund probes cannot respond to a φ shift; gradient and LRP give per-jet r 0.31–0.45 (DAS r not comparable). Conclusion: ParticleNet's W-versus-Z score responds the same way to a mass-preserving φ shift of either sign; a direction after the second graph layer reproduces about three quarters of each, but directions like it also exist in untrained networks, and it is not a copy of the axis-moment mass. The rebuilt neighbour graphs carry at most about 4 % of the response. A few dozen pooled channels kept alone reproduce the W/Z calls but not the 10-class calls | refines C5 (graphs ≤ 4 %); new C9; C7 analogue not supported as a trained m_A variable; adds a weight-shuffled control (ParT's P8 had an untrained control, 0.02–0.31 vs 0.58–0.69 trained; a weight-shuffled ParT control was not run) |

Strong class-share switches in 4 of 6 seeds (full s1 c2h2 105 GeV, full s2 c2h4 68 GeV, kin s1 c2h1 64 / c2h3 85 GeV, kin s2 c2h3 85 GeV, 0.85 → 0.11, amplitude 0.74 vs b2h8 0.79) | corrected reading (P11v): in all six retrained seeds, on two disjoint samples, φ + 0.06 moves true W to Z by +13.6 to +19.8 points; untrained networks −0.3 to +4.0. R lies between 0 and 1 in every seed, but untrained networks also give R −0.23 to 0.78, so R's value is not a training signature. S1 holds in 3/6 seeds and does not separate trained from untrained; S2 is structural. S3 fails in 6/6 (0.63–0.84); the seeds lie inside the untrained range (0.32–0.87); only the released 0.97–0.98 exceeds every untrained value. The released single block-1 head share 0.46 is not reproduced (seeds ≤ 0.23). Strong switch heads in 4/6 seeds, at different heads and masses; kin s2 c2h3 (85 GeV) is as large as b2h8. Seed, distinct-data scale (2M vs 100M jets) and total training (51M vs 512M samples) remain confounded. Conclusion: six Particle Transformers retrained from scratch all keep the released network's main behaviour on two test samples: rotating every particle about the jet axis, which leaves the jet mass unchanged, turns 14–20 % more true W jets into Z, while untrained networks barely respond. Inside, the retrained models split the mass response between the two class-attention layers at levels untrained networks already reach, whereas the released checkpoints send almost all of it through the first; sharp mass-switching heads appear in some retrained models at different heads and masses. Seed and training scale are not separated | supports C3s at the call level (0.3 → 0.7, reworded); refines C2a / C2b to the released checkpoints; S1 not a training test |

Arm c gives R 0. Planted arms take 79–83 % of a real mass change through the pair features, the released models 82–84 % through the per-particle angles. ParT with these inputs can learn a mostly rotation-invariant mass. Labels a and b coincide on 97.7 % of jets and the arms share initialisation and data order, so arm b does not test whether the label sets the mass read; "H_data strict excluded" is withdrawn. Source of the released models' larger R (10 classes, data size, training length, seed) not identified; planted R moves 0.12–0.24 across epochs. Arm s collapsed (its bars in p14_planted_p16.png are noise). Conclusion: Particle Transformers trained from scratch to flag a jet-mass window respond to a rotation that leaves the invariant mass unchanged at about 0.2 of their response to an equal real mass change, against about 0.75 for the released models, on fresh jets and with two read-outs. So the architecture with these inputs can learn a mostly rotation-invariant mass and does not force the axis-relative response. The axis-moment-mass label equals the invariant-mass label on almost every real jet, so whether the training target sets which mass is read is not shown, and which part of the released training produces the larger response is not identified | refines C10 (v1 pre-registration: R_a < 0.2 holds, R_b ≈ 1 not observed; H_arch excluded; reworded v2, 0.55); C3 reproduced on fresh rows; method validation: 1-d DAS and EAP did not separate arms whose labels coincide on 97.7 % |

Untrained-net DAS (seed 1018) emb / b4 / b8: scramble 0.33 / 0.53 / 0.52, soft5 0.51 / 0.63 / 0.62, phi 0.61 / 0.64 / 0.62; untrained IIA 0.45–0.95, above ParT_full in 44 of 55 cells. Difference-in-differences vs untrained (full): phi −0.20, dil −0.15, pT −0.18, per-particle scramble +0.18 [0.14, 0.22], soft5 +0.007 [−0.025, 0.040] (p 0.66; kin +0.11). Untrained mass R² margin 0.04–0.07 (seed 18), 0.085–0.13 (seed 1018). **Rerun 2** (_102, seed 2018, N 5,000): full-jet scramble (pair features also scrambled; 46.2 % calls changed vs 5.9 % per-particle only) IIA emb / b4 / b7 0.02 / 0.59 / 0.68; untrained flat 0.45 / 0.53 from b1; DiD +0.45 [0.43, 0.47] and +0.44 [0.42, 0.47] (full), +0.43 / +0.42 (kin) | corrected reading (P18v): N and log pT readable at the embedding (R² 0.99), the masses at b1 (0.94), no later increase; untrained ParT reaches 0.99 for N, pT and 0.80–0.89 for the masses (margin 0.04–0.13). Readable order (N, pT) emb → mass, tau21 b1 → tau32 b2–b4; H1's mass-last order not observed (fresh-row mass − prong t90 −0.38 / −0.42 sites). Only tau21 / tau32 read-outs depend on training (scramble gain 0.77–0.80 vs 0.00–0.05 untrained). 1-d DAS levels are not training evidence (untrained 0.45–0.95); training-specific are the depth profiles: fall with depth for pT, dilation, φ (DiD −0.15 to −0.20) and rise for the azimuth scramble (per-particle 0.01 → 0.38, DiD +0.15 to +0.18; full-jet 0.02 → 0.68 at b6–b7 vs flat 0.45–0.54 untrained, DiD +0.44). Soft-particle rise training-specific only in ParT_kin. The permuted-net control (rows permuted without the next layer's inputs, accuracy 10–12 %) is a weak null. Conclusion: in ParT, jet multiplicity and pT can be read from the input embedding and the jet mass from the first block on, but an untrained network of the same shape gives nearly the same read-outs, so these read-outs do not show learning and there is no mass-last stage. Prong-shape variables (tau21, tau32) are read only by the trained network, and the share of the response to randomising each particle's azimuth about the axis that one direction carries rises from about 0.02 at the embedding to about 0.7 by blocks 6–7, against a flat 0.5 in untrained networks. Which prong observable is used (tau21, tau32 or D2, which the edit changes together) is unresolved | refines C4 (v1 "readability follows algebraic depth" not supported as mass-last; reworded v2, 0.55); A8: no JEDI-like mass-last stage in ParT; adds the untrained-net DAS control (C7, C9 relevance) |

The soft-radial edit (D2 −4 %) moves ParT_full +0.31 vs the unedited jet (the reported +0.73 is vs a rotation that itself moves −0.42); through the mass-restoring dilation it raises the hard-particle mass by about 16 %, and a reference with that mass predicts +0.29 on average (not class by class). Block / head patching in blocks 5–8 gives the same profile and leading heads for prong edits and observable-neutral rotations, so it does not locate an observable-specific stage; DAS shows a training-specific late rise for E1 and E3 above their rotations and well below the full scramble. Conclusion: ParT does not respond to τ21, τ32 or D2 at fixed jet mass the way classifiers built on these variables do: narrowing the two prongs of W, Z and top jets makes ParT_full less W-like, where a (mass, τ21) classifier predicts the opposite, while QCD jets follow the classifier. The large response to moving soft particles inward is about +0.3 against the unedited jet and may come from the hard-particle mass the edit also raises. Over epochs 0 → 9: R(φ+) t 0.139 → 0.221, t2 0.067 → 0.251; R(φ−) t 0.734 → 0.470, t2 0.933 → 0.491 | corrected reading (P22v): the planted arms' R depends on the shift direction (0.18–0.26 for φ+, 0.41–0.49 for φ−, 0.21–0.26 for η+); the released models give 0.73–0.86 in all three. The pre-registered thesis range (R_t 0.6–0.8) is excluded in every direction; alt 1 (R_t ≈ R_a) holds for φ+ (t − a −0.01 to +0.06 over directions); alt 2 (R_u ≈ R_t) excluded (R_u ≤ 0.02 at equal accuracy, 99 % of the dilation response kept). R does not approach the released level over 10 epochs. Route share and R are in the same order only between planted and released models and for u, not within the planted arms (a2 R 0.263 > t 0.221, share 0.22 < 0.30). R_probe in the JSON is uncalibrated and unused. Bootstrap CIs cover jets only; seed spread 0.02–0.08. Conclusion: Particle Transformers trained on 1.5 million W, Z and QCD jets with the true labels respond to a mass-preserving shift of the jet axis only 0.2–0.5 times as strongly as to a matched real mass change, depending on the shift direction, against 0.73–0.86 for the released JetClass models in every direction, and they do not approach that level over training. Training with random axis offsets removes the response (ratio about 0.01) at the same accuracy, so the architecture does not force it. Which difference in the released training (100M jets, 10 classes or many more optimisation steps) produces the larger response is untested | weakens the node thesis (true labels at 1.5M do not reproduce the released R); refines C10 (v3: true labels and window labels both give R ≪ released; offset augmentation removes it); C3s: released response needs training beyond 3 classes at 1.5M |
| P23 | C3, C7, C5/C9 → C12 (new), D4, A8 (loop C round C5, exploratory) | is ParT's response to a rigid axis offset an even axis-moment (M2) term or a direction-dependent (odd, centroid-linear) term as in JEDI-linear; ParT vs ParticleNet | done; verified with corrections (P23v, confidence 0.62; jobs c-p23-verify, c-p23-verify2) | jobs anrunw-ai-c-p23-run (GPU 3,338 s, first attempt evicted), c-p23b-perjet (CPU), c-p23-smoke, c-p23-plot; scripts interp/p23_axis_surface.py, interp/p23b_perjet.py. Sample: test_20M W/Z/ZJetsToNuNu _105, 3,000 random rows each (default_rng(2305)), N 9,000. Models ParT_full/kin, P10 seeds s1/s2, PN_full/kin, two untrained ParT. Edits: rigid translation on a 9 × 9 grid \|c_i\| ≤ 0.08 rad (40 mirror pairs), 4 random directions at \|c\| 0.06, rotations 90/180/270°, matched dilation k_j. Read-out d = Δ(logit_Z − logit_W); odd/even from mirror pairs; gains g = b/κ (κ = response per unit ½dlog M2 under dilation). P1-row accuracy 85.93 / 74.83 % (kin; P4 74.46 %, unexplained), PN 84.57 / 70.34 %, seeds 83.6 / 83.5 %, untrained 12.7 / 11.9 %. g_odd vs g_even: ParT_full 0.68 [0.56, 0.79] vs 0.66 [0.64, 0.67] (p 0.71); ParT_kin 0.02 vs 0.50; s1 0.33 vs 0.59; s2 0.63 vs 0.74; PN_full 0.52 vs 0.27; PN_kin 0.71 vs 0.15. Odd variance share: ParT 0.064–0.110, PN 0.16–0.18, untrained 0.57–0.67. φ + 0.06 mean (ParT_full): total 0.760, even 0.744. Even R²: pooled 0.31, per-jet κ_j 0.72, per-jet quadratic 0.87. Per-jet odd gradient vs own pT centroid cos 0.132 vs null 0.125 (p 0.07). Long-vs-short even: ParT_full −0.098, PN −0.63 / −1.53. R to matched dilation ParT_full 0.78–0.82, PN_full 0.47–0.58. W → Z (ParT_full) φ + 0.06 10.4 → 31.0 %, rot90 13.2 %. **P23v rerun** (_107, seed 2399, W/Z N 4,000): ParT_full odd share 0.063 [0.059, 0.066], g_o 0.68, g_e 0.65, φ + 0.06 total 0.755 / even 0.749, W → Z 9.9 → 29.3 %, R 0.79–0.84; κ_j control even R² own 0.72, other-jet 0.10, rotation scale 0.11, none 0.32; Σ pT\|r+c\|^b per-jet R² b = 2 0.88, b = 1 0.82, b = 3 0.85 (untrained b = 2 0.92). **P23v2** (_108, seed 2311, N 4,000): corr(Po, tanh\|η_jet\|) −0.944, M1η < 0 in 98.3 % of jets; g_odd raw → axis-only inputs ParT_full 0.69 → 0.59, PN_full 0.66 → 0.57 (Holm 0.005), odd share change ≤ 0.009; per-jet φ-axis odd share ParT_full 0.075 [0.071, 0.081], ParT_kin 0.100, PN_full 0.180, PN_kin 0.169, untrained 0.650; odd-gradient cos: ParT_kin vs leading particle 0.130 (null −0.021, p 0.001), ParT_full vs unweighted centroid 0.053 (0.007, p 0.004), PN_full unweighted 0.185, leading −0.201; within-jet s.d. across directions at \|c\| 0.06 0.33 vs mean 0.75 (ParT_full); per-jet even-anisotropy median 0.15 (ParT_full), 0.47 (PN_full) | corrected reading (P23v): about 92 % of each jet's response variance to a rigid φ shift is symmetric under c → −c (92–94 % over the grid); P23's "98 % of the mean" is near-guaranteed by φ-reflection symmetry and is not used. Among Σ pT\|r+c\|^b, b = 2 (M2) fits best for trained nets, and the per-jet size follows the jet's own dilation response (R² 0.72 vs 0.10); a quadratic symmetric shape alone is generic (untrained fits it too). The antisymmetric part (6–10 %) does not follow the pT-weighted centroid but weakly the leading particle (ParT_kin) or unweighted centroid (ParT_full). P23's pooled g_odd is a gain on a regressor 94 % determined by jet \|η\| (13–27 % from η-shift energy changes), not a centroid gain. ParticleNet antisymmetric share 16–19 %, aligned with the unweighted centroid and away from the leading particle, and its symmetric part follows the dilation less (R² 0.37 / 0.18). At fixed \|c\| the response varies with direction (s.d. 0.33 vs mean 0.75), unexplained by M2 or the jet's long axis; "10 %" understated. Conclusion: when all of a jet's particles are shifted by a small angle with the mass unchanged, about 93 % of ParT's per-jet response is the same for a shift and its mirror image, and its size follows the jet's response to a real widening of its angles, as expected if ParT reads the angular second moment about the stored axis. ParticleNet has a larger mirror-asymmetric part (about 18 %), and in neither network does that part follow the pT-weighted centroid that JEDI-linear uses. A substantial dependence on the shift's direction at fixed size remains unexplained | refines C3 (axis response is an even, M2-like term per jet); new C12 (0.62); bears on C7's 1/7 gap (the φ shift acts through the even term; gap not explained by an odd term); C5 / A8: response shape differs between ParT and ParticleNet |
| P26 | C3s / P20 (data scale) → C13 (new), C3 (loop C round C6, exploratory) | does Sophon (ParT architecture, released weights, trained on JetClass-II with 188 classes, mass-decorrelated) keep the axis-relative mass response | done; verified (P26v, confidence 0.75; job c-p26-verify) | jobs anrunw-ai-c-p26-run2 (final), c-p26-run, c-p26-smoke, c-p26-plot; scripts interp/p26_sophon_axis.py, interp/sophon_part.py (copied from weaver-core), interp/p26_fetch.sh, interp/p26_plot.py; weights /j-jepa-vol/aaron-interp/models/sophon (jet-universe/sophon model.pt, 2.305 M parameters, 8 + 2 blocks; ONNX check max \|Δsoftmax\| 6e-6, top-1 agreement 100 % on 200 jets). Inputs per JetClassII_full.yaml (17 features, four-vectors × 500/jet_pT). Read-out d = log p(Z group: X→bb, cc, ss) − log p(W group: X→bc, cs, bq, cq, sq) (X→qq excluded); ridge probe of log m_inv on the 128-d class token. Sample A: JetClass test_20M W/Z/ZJetsToNuNu _112, 5,000 random rows per class (seed 2601). Sample B: JetClass-II Res2P_0250–0253 + QCD_0350, pT 500–1000 GeV, 70 < m_X < 100 GeV, N 5,496 (seed 2602). Edits: φ + 0.06, matched dilation, whole-jet rotation null, φ with ΔR or Δφ unedited (routes), WTA re-axis with same-length random axis move and ΔR-keeping rotation as controls. References ParT_full/kin, two untrained Sophon inits; permutation tests, Holm over 21. P1 accuracy ParT 0.8593 / 0.7447. Sophon mapped 3-class A 0.626 [0.618, 0.634], B 0.752; resonance vs QCD 0.831 / 0.867; 188-class top-1 on B 0.4995; ParT_full mapped A 0.824, B 0.710. Dilation Δd on W jets / Z−W separation: Sophon 0.016 (A), ≈ 0 (B); ParT_full 0.278. R output: Sophon −0.256 [−0.308, −0.207] (A), −0.006 (B); ParT_full 0.801 / 0.759; ParT_kin 0.734 / 0.626; rotation null ≤ 0.016. R_probe: Sophon 0.125 [0.109, 0.140] (A), 0.252 (B); ParT_full 0.776 / 0.971; ParT_kin 1.507 / 0.786; untrained 0.844 / 0.563. W → Z under φ: Sophon 24.3 → 23.5 % (A, Holm p 0.012); ParT_full 11.5 → 32.9 %; ParT_kin 17.9 → 41.7 %. WTA top-1 changed (WTA / random axis move / rotation): Sophon A 7.3 / 6.6 / 16.0 %, B 6.8 / 6.1 / 18.3 %; ParT_full 21.8 / 28.2 / 8.4 %; ParT_kin 34.4 / 40.7 / 9.5 %. **P26v rerun** (A' _113 seed 2699 N 15,000; B' Res2P_0254–0258 + QCD_0351 seed 2698 N 6,000): Sophon acc3 0.622 / 0.737; dil/sep 0.018 / −0.001; R −0.322 / −0.026; R_probe 0.179 [0.163, 0.197] / 0.232; probe calibration 0.46 / 0.56 (untrained 0.15, R² 0.26); probe-free token projection R_h −0.298 [−0.310, −0.286] / −0.121 (ParT_full 0.843 / 0.89); route split of the dilation probe response four-vector / angular 0.40 / 0.57 (A'), 0.47 / 0.50 (B'), ParT_full 0.05 / 0.95; Sophon W → Z 25.4 → 23.8 % (p 1.5e-7); WTA / random move / rotation 7.2 / 6.7 / 16.6 % (A', p 0.03), 6.9 / 6.7 / 17.7 % (B', p 0.50); paired bootstrap R_probe Sophon − ParT_full −0.62 [−0.64, −0.60] (A'), −0.75 (B'), R_h −1.14 / −1.02, all Holm 0.008 | corrected reading (P26v): Sophon's flavour-group W/Z output barely changes under a real 12 % mass increase (1.6–1.8 % of the Z−W separation on JetClass, ≈ 0 on JetClass-II), so output R is a ratio of small numbers. Its class-token mass probe moves under φ + 0.06 at 0.13–0.25 of its dilation movement (ParT_full 0.78–0.98, untrained 0.83 with poor calibration); the probe-free token projection gives −0.30 / −0.12. 50–57 % of the probe's dilation response enters through the per-particle angular inputs (ParT_full 95 %), so Sophon's size reading is largely insensitive to the axis offset but is not a pure four-vector invariant-mass reading ("mostly invariant mass representation" withdrawn). WTA re-axing changes Sophon's calls at the level of a same-length random axis move; ΔR-keeping rotations change more, so that rotation is not an informative control for Sophon. Its output moves under the φ shift opposite to the dilation (2.6× the dilation size). Scale, mass decorrelation and pT-normalised four-vectors are confounded. Conclusion: Sophon, a Particle Transformer released after training on the much larger JetClass-II dataset, hardly changes its W/Z output when the jet mass is really increased, so the output-level axis test has nothing to measure. Inside, its mass-like read-out moves about 0.2 as much under a mass-preserving axis shift as under a real mass change, against 0.8–1.0 for the JetClass ParT; about half of its size reading still uses the axis-relative angles, so it reads jet size in a way that mostly ignores where the axis sits rather than reading the invariant mass. Whether data scale, mass decorrelation or pT-normalised inputs causes the difference is open | refines C3s/C3: the axis-relative mass reading is not a property of the ParT architecture; new C13 (0.7); node thesis (R 0.5–1.0, WTA ≥ 10 %) excluded; the alternative's "dilation response kept" not met |
| P27 | A8, C12, P12 → C14 (new), C5 (loop C round C6, exploratory) | per-particle kernel of the axis response in ParT and ParticleNet: does each particle's response to a small radial move follow ∂M2 = 2 pT r, or JEDI-style pT-rank windows / thresholds | done; verified with corrections (P27v, confidence 0.6; jobs c-p27-verify, c-p27-verify-ana2) | jobs anrunw-ai-c-p27-run2 (GPU 400 s), c-p27-ana (CPU 76 s); script interp/p27_particle_kernel.py, verify interp/p27v_particle_kernel_verify.py. Models ParT_full/kin, PN_full/kin; controls untrained ParT_full (seed 2701), ParT_kin with rows of every weight tensor permuted. Sample: test_20M WToQQ_106 + ZToQQ_106, 2,000 random rows each (rng 2701), N 4,000 jets, 97,370 particles with pT share > 0.5 %. Edits (dr 0.01 rad, pT kept, all inputs incl. pair features rebuilt): each particle outward / inward; control (a) same particle rotated about the axis at fixed r; 6 random pairs per jet; control (b) total displacement spread as dr/5 over 5 random particles; all particles outward (calibration); unedited re-run (floor ≤ 1.1e-6). d = Δ(ℓ_Z − ℓ_W). Forms (global coefficients): M2 (x = dM2/M2), M2 with score-dependent gain, M2 per rank window, per-window (z, r, zr), pT-share hinges, radius hinges, pT-only, r-only, boosted tree; targets outward, odd (out − in)/2, rad − az; 5-fold held-out R², binned R², BIC; 1,000 jet bootstraps; Holm over 108. P1 10-class accuracy ParT 85.93 / 74.83 %, PN 84.57 / 70.34 %; W+Z sample 75.1 / 62.5 / 73.0 / 59.2 %. Values ParT_full / ParT_kin / PN_full / PN_kin: mean d_rad 0.027 [0.025, 0.029] / 0.024 / 0.022 / 0.020; even share of per-particle variance 0.47 / 0.45 / 0.52 / 0.24; \|az\|/\|rad\| 0.70 / 0.73 / 0.81 / 0.71; signed azimuthal mean 0.000 (p 0.44–0.99). Held-out R² M2 odd part 0.030 [0.024, 0.036] / 0.040 / 0.031 / 0.068, outward 0.019 / 0.035 / 0.018 / 0.078; largest rank-window / threshold gain over M2 +0.012 (PN_kin, Holm p 5e-12); tree 0.064 / 0.110 / 0.067 / 0.223; binned R² M2 0.93 / 0.91 / 0.95 / 0.91 (untrained −3.2). Pair additivity 0.947 [0.900, 0.978] / 0.968 / 0.939 / 0.956; sum of singles / whole-jet 1.003 [0.951, 1.063] / 1.062 / 0.888 / 1.017. Untrained mean \|d\| 0.0014 (r-only kernel); permuted ParT_kin R² ≈ 0 for every form (calls 99.8 % W). **P27v rerun** (WToQQ_107 + ZToQQ_107, 1,000 random rows each, rng 2777, 48,628 particles): mean d_rad 0.029 [0.027, 0.032] / 0.024 / 0.025 / 0.021; even share 0.46 / 0.44 / 0.49 / 0.24; held-out R² M2 outward 0.028 / 0.039 / 0.022 / 0.082; pt_only − M2 −0.001 to −0.006; pt_only binned R² 0.96–1.00 (M2 0.91–0.95). Within-z-decile slope on dM2/M2 relative to the M2-only slope: 0.65 / 0.60 / 0.78 / 0.63 (outward), 0.72 / 0.69 / 0.74 / 0.74 (odd); ratio 0 rejected p < 1e-17, ratio 1 rejected p_Holm 4.6e-3 to 1e-22. Route split (additivity R² 0.985–0.995): ParT pair-input-only route carries 0.96 / 0.98 of the even mean square (\|az\|/\|rad\| 0.93–0.94); per-particle-only route 0.75 / 0.80 of the mean response, even share 0.01–0.04; ParticleNet 97–99 % through per-particle (EdgeConv) features, not kNN coordinates. Even/odd variance ratio at 0.003 vs 0.01 rad: 0.38 / 1.72 / 0.43 / 0.69 (smooth prediction 0.09, p 2e-5 to 5e-69); ParT per-particle route 0.12–0.14 | corrected reading (P27v): the mean single-particle response rises with pT share and, at fixed pT share, with r; the within-z slope on dM2/M2 is 0.6–0.8 of the M2-only slope (both 0 and 1 excluded), so "in proportion to M2" is too strong. Per particle, M2, pT-only, r-only, rank-window and threshold forms all reach held-out R² ≤ 0.08; about half of the per-particle variance is even under out vs in and tracks the fixed-r rotation response; in ParT this component is carried by the pair inputs (96–98 %) and is not smooth (does not shrink as step²). In ParticleNet it goes through the per-particle EdgeConv features. No signed azimuthal term in any model. The rank-window vs M2 comparison (ΔR² ≤ 0.012) is made where every form explains < 10 %, so it is weak evidence on A8; rank windows were not fitted on the smooth ParT per-particle route (M2 R² 0.09–0.14 there). Pair additivity, calibration and the untrained / permuted controls were not rerun. Conclusion: moving one particle slightly away from the jet axis shifts the W-versus-Z score of ParT and ParticleNet, and on average the shift grows with the particle's momentum share and its distance from the axis, at about 0.6–0.8 of the steepness that exact proportionality to the axis moment M2 would give. Particle by particle no simple formula predicts the shift, and JEDI-style momentum-rank windows do no better than M2. In ParT most of the scatter comes from the pair features between particles and responds in a non-smooth way; what that pair term reads, and whether rank windows appear in the smooth per-particle part, is untested | new C14 (0.55); refines C12 (M2 reading holds for sums and means, not per particle); weakens A8 at the per-particle level (weak evidence); C5: no per-particle signed azimuthal term in ParticleNet |

Full-swap (source-call) baseline: M0 0.34–0.35, M2 0.60–0.61, Δ 0.33–0.34, W−Z 0.51, Da 0.61. Angle-only Δ edit Da (angles × c, M0 and pT fixed): Δ-subspace 0.19–0.43 on Da, below the full swap by 0.18–0.42 in 9/9 cells (p 0.001); h4 k4 Δ-DAS 0.61 on Δ vs 0.42 on Da (+0.19 [+0.16, +0.22]); Da-DAS 0.75, M2-DAS on Da 0.73; overlap M2–Da 0.68 (random 0.06), Δ–Da 0.27; pT-scale and angle-only Δ edits give the same call on 33 % of Da's call changes. Untrained vs full swap (x10-verify3u): seed 0 M0 0.45 (DAS 0.02–0.37), M2 0.39 (0.05–0.24); seed 2 M0 0.44 (0.18–0.37), M2 0.49 (0.12–0.34); trained above it in 15/18 M0/M2 cells, untrained below in 36/36; W−Z: untrained seed 0 S 0.48/0.52 vs trained 0.43/0.35, seed 2 h5 k1 0.69 vs 0.30 | corrected reading (X10v): subspaces of 1–2 dims at S, h4, h5 reproduce the call changes of M0 and M2 input edits on 0.66–0.87 of call-changing pairs (random ≤ 0.14), with the range clip as part of the intervention. Against the full-swap baseline: M0 +0.5 (S, h4), M2 +0.05 to +0.17, W−Z +0.15 to +0.30 at h4/h5 (k ≥ 2), not above at S. The "Δ" subspaces reproduce the response to a global pT rescale, not an angle-only edit of the same Δ (that one follows the M2 subspace), so Δ is not shown as one variable at any stage. Random subspaces do not separate trained from untrained networks; the full-swap baseline does for M0 and M2, not for W−Z. Conclusion: swapping one or two learned directions of JEDI-linear's object layer between two jets changes its decision as an edit of total pT or of the pT-weighted squared spread about the axis does, on most decision changes, far above random directions and, for total pT, far above simply giving the jet the other jet's class. The combination ½log(M0·M2) is not found as one separate quantity: the directions found for it follow a pT rescale, while the same change made with angles runs through the spread directions. How much the spread directions add beyond the full-swap baseline is small (+0.05 to +0.17) | refines B2m (variables exist as subspaces; named-formula basis not needed) and A4 (M0, M2 at S); weakens the A4 product / stage clause (Δ not a separate variable); untrained control: random-subspace test not sufficient |

Fit sample(5000, 0) (16,666 train / 8,334 dev), test sample(5000, 29) minus overlap (22,551). Layers: JEDI S (19 live), h4 (55), Deep Sets dsets-d64 s0 S (59), PFN latent (30). Bases: native, 20 raw rotations (primary null), 10 z-scored rotations, PCA, ReLU SAE 2×/4× (dev FVU ≤ 0.10). Named = held-out R² ≥ 0.9 for ≤ 8-term MARS on 97 rank-window observables. Edit test: pT or angles × 1.1 in the top term's window, slope CI ⊂ [0.8, 1.2]. Accuracy JEDI 80.87 %, Deep Sets 80.57 %, PFN 81.30 %. Fraction named native vs rotations [min, max] / SAE2× / SAE4×: JEDI S 0.79 (15/19) vs [0.32, 0.63] / 0.16 / 0.21; JEDI h4 0.55 vs [0.20, 0.36] / 0.09 / 0.11; Deep Sets 0.29 vs [0.00, 0.12] / 0.07 / 0.03; PFN 0.10 vs [0.07, 0.20] / 0.10. Native − rotation mean: JEDI S +0.35 [0.34, 0.36], h4 +0.25, Deep Sets +0.21 (p 0.048 floor, Holm 0.29); PFN −0.02 (p 0.71). SAE − native: JEDI S −0.63 / −0.58, h4 −0.45 / −0.44, Deep Sets −0.22 / −0.25 (Holm 0.028); PFN 0.00. Edit pass: JEDI S 7/19 (S0 0.85, S1 1.16, S29 0.91, S40, S43, S50, S51; S19 0.13, S30 0.48 fail) vs rotations 0.22; mismatched-formula control 0/15. Median R² native: JEDI S 0.955, h4 0.906, Deep Sets 0.663, PFN 0.694. **X11v rerun** pass 1 (sample(4000, 101/103)): JEDI S 0.789 vs 200 rotations max 0.632 (p 0.005); h4 0.564 vs 40 rotations max 0.364; generic 380-observable library 0.737 vs 50 rotations max 0.579; SAE − native −0.63; among named directions native vs rotated pass the edit test at 0.47 vs 0.50 (p 0.62). Pass 2 (fit sample(3000, 401), test sample(3000, 409), 14,141 jets; 80.84 %): JEDI S 0.789 (same 15 units) vs 50 rotations mean 0.397, max 0.526 (p 0.020); Deep Sets 0.288 vs 10 rotations max 0.119. Untrained float networks: JEDI architecture 0/56 and 1/57 named, Deep Sets 0/46, rotations ≤ 0.065 | On a fixed library of rank-window pT/angle observables, JEDI-linear's S units are named at 0.79 on three samples, above every one of 250 random rotations; h4 0.55–0.56 above 40 rotations; SAE and PCA directions are named less often in every model. Deep Sets pooled units also beat their rotations (0.29); the PFN latent does not. The edit-test excess over rotations follows from the naming excess (named native vs rotated 0.47 vs 0.50). Untrained architectures have almost no nameable units, so readability needs training; whether the basis preference is learned or architectural is not decided (control at floor). Intervals hold formulas fixed. Conclusion: JEDI-linear's pooled object units can each be written as a short formula of pT and angular-distance sums over pT-rank windows for 15 of 19 units, on three independent jet samples, while no random mixing of the same units reaches that rate. A sparse autoencoder makes the layer less, not more, readable by this measure. Deep Sets units also prefer their own basis at a lower rate; the PFN latent does not. Open: whether the unit basis is preferred because of training or because of the per-particle ReLU-sum architecture, and how much the SAE result depends on the additive hinge scorer | supports A11 (new A11b, 0.75); supports A3 (S units nameable); weakness 4 addressed for JEDI-linear; basis preference not unique to JEDI-linear (Deep Sets) |

Retrained ratios at the official 13|14 vs 11|12 edge 0.20–0.90 (sign-flip p ≥ 0.60; official 5.18); HGQ-50 s0, s1 peak at their own mask edges near ranks 40/42 (index 3.5/4.4 vs official 2.86). Matched real-change calibration flow(T)/flow(dilM2) (angles × √(M2(T)/M2), mean 1.32): official 1.22 [1.20, 1.25], HGQ 1.02–1.38, float 0.69 [0.64, 0.74] / 1.30 [1.23, 1.38] (network-level p 0.24); W→Z under a real W→Z dilation ×91.19/80.38: HGQ 0.795–0.844, float 0.854–0.855; dilM2 W→Z float 0.26–0.30 vs HGQ 0.49–0.62. M1-held 0.19–0.24 vs M2-held 0.03–0.19 (McNemar p ≤ 1e-11, 9/9). Network-level permutation: radial hinge all usable HGQ vs float p 0.048, primary arm p 0.17; flow(T) p 0.048 | corrected reading (X12v): the translation W→Z response removed by holding Σ pT r² fixed recurs in 4 of 5 usable retrained HGQ networks (HGQ-150 s0 passes only because its unedited rate equals the threshold). Float networks give 2–3× lower W→Z under T, but relative to a real widening with the same M0 and M2 they respond as much (0.69–1.30 vs 1.02–1.38, p 0.24) and to a real W→Z mass shift as strongly; the lower W→Z is where the float networks put the Z boundary for ~105 GeV-equivalent jets, not weaker use of the uncentred spread. No retrained network reproduces the official 13|14 swap response (0.20–0.90 vs 5.18), although all quantized ones form zero-bit slot masks. Purity follows from the 13–20-entry sparsity HGQ produces. Radial hinge objects are more frequent with quantization in every network; network-level p 0.048 (all usable) / 0.17 (primary). Conclusion: retrained copies of JEDI-linear, with or without quantization-aware training, read the jet's mass through the pT-weighted spread about the stored axis, as the official network does, so that behaviour belongs to the architecture and inputs, not to one checkpoint. The official network's strong response at the 14-hardest-particle boundary does not appear in any retrained copy, so it is specific to that checkpoint or to its much longer training. "Pure" first-layer cuts follow from how few weights quantization-aware training keeps. The retrained quantized copies are 1–4 points less accurate and trained about 10× fewer epochs | refines A10m (mass reading shared by HGQ and float retrainings; "weaker without quantization" not supported); weakens A1 generality (window effect specific to the official checkpoint); A1q reworded (sparsity, not purity); A3 radial excess with quantization at network-level p 0.048 |

JEDI aug T − Tr: s0 0.053 [0.030, 0.076], s2 0.276 [0.250, 0.299]; T − Tr also in stored networks (Deep Sets 0.14 / 0.16, official 0.16, EFN s0 −0.18). Per network, aug accuracy loss up to 1.13 points (Deep Sets s1) | corrected reading (X9wv): PFN, EFN and Deep Sets trained on stored-axis inputs send 0.35–0.71 of W jets to Z under a 0.06 rad rigid translation; the same architectures trained with translation augmentation (\|c\| ≤ 0.08 rad) are at the rotation-null level (≤ 0.012) at ≤ 1.13 points accuracy per network; recentred networks give exactly 0 by construction. A 0.12 rad translation sends 0.51–0.84 of the augmented networks' W jets to Z, so augmentation suppresses the response only for offsets inside the trained range; X9w's "mass read about the jet's own centroid" holds only for recentred networks. All networks respond to dilM2. In retrained JEDI-linear HGQ-50, aug reduced the 0.06 rad flow to the null level in 1 of 3 seeds; the other two keep 0.38–0.50 and lost 6–14 accuracy points, so architecture vs under-training is not separated. The representation and interaction 2×2 effects are fixed by how recentred inputs are built; only stored vs aug is an empirical test. Conclusion: in three standard set networks the uncentred mass response comes from the training data: when the networks are trained on jets randomly shifted by up to 0.08 rad they stop responding to such shifts, at a cost of about one accuracy point at most. They do not learn a shift-proof mass, since a larger shift (0.12 rad) still moves most W jets to Z. Whether the same holds for JEDI-linear is open, because its augmented retrainings lost several accuracy points and two of three kept the response | refines A10m (input-format / training-data origin in PFN, EFN, Deep Sets within the augmented range; new claim A10r 0.55); JEDI-linear part unresolved |

JEDI vs F1 under A′ McNemar p 0.60 (vs F2 p 0.27). Accuracy drop A′ vs rot: JEDI 1.45 [0.97, 1.90], F1 1.14 [0.65, 1.61], F0 0.00. τ21 and D2 add 0.22 and 0.42 points of accuracy over mass and radial features (78.71 → 78.93 → 79.13 %). rot2 − rot null for JEDI −0.59 [−0.99, −0.21] points (rotation-draw spread ±0.5 points, not in the jet bootstrap) | corrected reading (E24v): under the scramble JEDI-linear changes 7.1–7.2 % of calls (rotation 4.0–4.3 %), the same share as a boosted tree that uses τ21 (6.9 %, McNemar p 0.60), because prong shape adds little beyond mass and radial shape on these jets; the uncalibrated 20–40 % alternative is not excluded by this test. JEDI's excess over rotation (2.8 points) lies between the mass-and-radial tree (1.1) and the τ21 tree (6.4), and its accuracy loss (1.45 points) equals the τ21 tree's. Latent results hold: the named radial S objects change ≤ 0.19 sd; the excess passes mostly through the context-only units S58, S31, which read the jet context g. Deep Sets and PFN change 7.3–8.7 % of calls, so call-level near-invariance does not separate JEDI-linear from them; at the latent level JEDI's S moves more under scramble than under rotation (0.47 vs 0.21 of its spread), Deep Sets and PFN less. Conclusion: randomising each particle's direction around the jet axis destroys the two-prong pattern of W jets and changes about 7 % of JEDI-linear's calls; a classifier built explicitly on the two-prong variable τ21 changes the same share, because prong shape adds little beyond mass and radial shape here. So this test cannot tell whether JEDI-linear uses prong structure; its extra change over rotation and its accuracy loss sit between a classifier that ignores prong shape and one that uses it. The named radial objects barely move; the change runs through two units that read only a jet-level summary | refines A3 (radial S objects ≤ 0.19 sd under the scramble); weakens the planned object-vs-Deep-Sets separation at call level (Deep Sets, PFN alike); new claims A3z (0.6) and A13 (azimuthal dependence at fixed moments, 0.5) |

Pass 3 (b6-verify3, B6 jets, all four networks, 815 s, peak 1,151 MiB): trans devsc JEDI −1.34, Deep Sets +0.23 [−0.81, 1.18], PFN +0.81, tf +1.59 [0.78, 2.47] (Holm 0.008); oracle −2.26 / −2.36 / +0.06 / −1.72; m2h devsc +2.01 / +0.43 / +0.10 / +1.69. Flipped-set Jaccard on trans (JEDI): GBDT lr 0.2 0.72–0.73, CART d7 0.71, prog30 0.63. Larger GBDT / MLP (4–124× bits) 5–12 points higher trans agreement | corrected reading (B6v): at about 13 kbit the blind program agrees with the networks' call changes under the 0.06 rad translation about as often as a tree, GBDT or MLP of at most the same bits selected on dev jets: −1.3 to +1.7 points over three samples in JEDI-linear and PFN, +0.2 (Deep Sets) and +1.6 (transformer) on B6 jets; with test-edit (oracle) selection the baseline is 0–2.4 points higher in all four. The 2.8–8.1-point lead over B6's single lr 0.2 GBDT comes from an under-fitted baseline and depends on requiring the same new call (that GBDT has higher flipped-set Jaccard). Under the M2-held translation the program leads in JEDI-linear on all three samples (+1.7 to +2.0, Holm ≤ 6e-5) and in the transformer on B6 jets (+1.7), not in PFN or Deep Sets; every surrogate ≤ 14 kbit is within about 3 points of never-flip there. Conclusion: a short list of if-statements on sums over pT-ranked particle windows follows how four different networks change their calls under a small jet shift about as well as tuned boosted trees or decision trees of the same size, with differences up to about 2 points either way depending on the jet sample. The earlier lead came from comparing against one under-fitted boosted-tree setting. The extraction method therefore transfers to Deep Sets, a particle-flow network and a transformer at tree-level fidelity per bit; an advantage in fidelity is not shown, and symbolic regression is untested | weakens B4 (0.25 → 0.15); supports transfer of the pipeline at baseline-level fidelity (new claim B6t 0.7); B3 unchanged |

Untrained seeds 3–8 constant | Corrected reading (E25v): the 16-feature first layer reads angles only through ΔR (10/17 live cuts, 8 mixed with pT) and one rotated-frame coordinate, zero weight on Δη, Δφ, η, φ; the share of mixed cuts equals random placement among active units (E25's "more mixed than random placement" withdrawn). A 0.06 rad translation turns 48–49 % of true W jets into Z (rotation 1.3–1.5 %), carried entirely by the ΔR column (ΔR held 1.2–1.4 %), close to an M2-matched ΔR dilation (0.98 at 0.03 rad, 1.05–1.07 at 0.06; "equals" withdrawn); an M2-preserving ΔR scramble gives 3.1 %, so the response follows Σ pT ΔR² about the stored axis; W→Z not monotone in shift size; no untrained baseline (8/9 redraws constant). Route (a) mass via ΔR fits; (b) half-planes and (c) absolute coordinates excluded. Conclusion: The more accurate 16-feature JEDI-linear does not build angular information from half-plane cuts; its first layer reads each particle's distance from the stored jet axis and ignores the separate Δη and Δφ inputs. Its mass-like response is still not recentred: moving all particles 0.06 rad off the axis turns about half of W jets into Z calls, through the distance column alone, and the response follows the pT-weighted spread of the particles about the axis. There is no usable untrained baseline, and where the mass variable forms in the later layers was not tested | refines A1x (half-plane construction specific to 3-feature inputs; new A1f 0.75), A4 (0.92 → 0.93), A10m (0.85 → 0.87) |

The quadrupole name is not a complete causal account of S58 (a 180° turn, which leaves quadrupole sums unchanged, moves S58 by 40 % of its 90° response; named θ explains 10 %, exact θ 72 %). S31's edit response is shaped by the per-slot l3 quantizer. S-level R² (0.79 / 0.63) limited by naming and quantization. Conclusion: The two summary units that carry JEDI-linear's context response do not look at individual particles; they read only the jet-level summary, which is built from plain counts of particles on either side of fixed lines. Their thresholds follow how far the particle-count centre sits from the axis (S31) and how elongated the particle-count distribution is (S58), fixed-frame shapes rather than rotation-invariant jet quantities. The elongation name does not account for all of S58's response when the jet is turned by 180°, and the network's per-slot rounding shapes S31's response | refines A3z (naming done; new A3c 0.65), A13 (fixed-frame context consistent with azimuth dependence) |

Instrumented, a1/a2 is 0.77 at S (equal to S-type radial sums, 0.69), 0.97 at h4, 0.86–0.87 at h5 and z (0.90–0.95 with a \|c\|² term), undetermined at the margin. E23w's "0.4–0.8 at every stage", "toward 1 from h4", "rises with \|c\|" and "odd shrinks 8–14× on bulk" are estimator properties; E23v's 1.34–1.82 odd gain not reproduced. Product test: no z unit or margin outside a wide random-pair band (not rerun); stage of any product not located. Conclusion: When naturally off-centre jets are shifted sideways, JEDI-linear weighs the part of the change that depends on the jet's centre offset about as much as the part that depends on the shift size from its dense layers on, once a measurement bias is removed; only the first summed stage weighs it less, as its radial sums predict. Most of the direction-dependent part of the response follows the plain (unweighted) centre of the particles rather than the momentum-weighted one. Other S units that carry the same S-level odd power pass on about twice as much downstream, so most of the odd response, including the margin's, goes through the particle-reading S units. With M2 held, moving only the hard 14 changes the margin about 2× more than moving only the soft particles (on top/gluon-dominated feasible jets; 1.03 without the hold). The odd response at S–z follows Σr² rather than M2, mainly because a shift changes Σr² 9× more. Conclusion: When all particles of a jet are shifted, the part of the network's response that depends on the shift direction passes only partly through the two context units that sum particle counts on each side of the axis (S31 and S58): about a quarter in the later layers and none in the final W-versus-Z score. Other second-round units that respond equally strongly pass on more of their response, so most of the direction-dependent response goes through the units that read individual particles. Which of those units carry it, and how it behaves on W and Z jets specifically, is still open | weakens the A4d/A3c link (S31/S58 majority route excluded); A4d 0.6 (=), A3c 0.65 (=), A4n 0.5 (=) |

Under translation W calls pass through Z (peak 0.87 at 0.045 rad) into top and q/g; an M2-matched ΔR dilation reproduces the aggregate W→Z count, dilations matched on Σ pT r or Σ r do not; per jet 3–26 % of calls differ. The trained flow exceeds all 10 non-trained baselines (p 0.091, the n = 10 floor). Conclusion: In the more accurate 16-feature network, the total pT and the pT-weighted spread of particles about the jet axis are each held as a separate internal direction already in the second pooled layer, just as in the 3-feature network; the two networks differ only in how the combined mass-like quantity develops in the next layer. Shifting all particles off the axis turns W jets first into Z and then into top or quark/gluon calls, and a stretch of the particle distances that changes the spread by the same amount gives the same number of W→Z changes, while stretches matched on other radial sums do not. R_rad near 1 is mostly a property of the edit (true-mass read-out 0.80, axis-moment read-out 1.00); across networks R_rad ≈ 0.80 + 0.33 R, so R is close to the share of the log-m read-out tied to the stored axis, the rest from shift-invariant quantities (pairwise distances or centroid recentring, not separated). The response differs from rotation in all 56 (negative in 2). Family explains R among JEDI-linear, PFN, EFN, Deep Sets (p 0.006), not when attention models are included (p 0.33). Seed differences below about 0.15 are not reproducible. Conclusion: Every one of the 56 trained networks reacts to a shift of the whole jet that leaves the true mass unchanged, so none of them computes the invariant mass alone. How strongly they react matches how much of their mass estimate is measured from the stored jet axis rather than from quantities that do not move with a shift, such as distances between particles. That share is set by the network type for the set-based networks, but in transformer-type networks it differs between training runs of the same design, by a factor of about six in one case. Which internal parts compute the shift-independent share, and whether it is particle-pair distances or a recentring, is still open | refines A10 (=), A10m (=); new claim A10s (0.45); X13's cancellation mechanism and "architecture labels explain nothing" not kept |

Holm: 46 program-ahead, 9 baseline-ahead of 144. **B6wv rerun** (fit sample(4000, 5301), test sample(2000, 5401), N 9,296; JEDI 80.55 %, PFN 81.22 %): K8 vs PySR (JEDI) +5.51 [4.17, 6.68], vs gplearn +7.58 (JEDI), +6.12 (PFN); m2h +10.9 to +21.9; PySR c17 / c23 57.8 / 57.1 % vs program 54.2 % (JEDI); MARS on standard observables trans 41.1–44.2 % (never-flip 44.4 %), program lead +12.5 to +19.5; GBDT on standard observables +10.9 to +11.9; vs rank-window baselines K16 +8.47 [7.43, 9.49] / K30 −5.74 [−6.70, −4.80] (JEDI), +1.36 / −2.84 (PFN); specificity −0.47 to +0.18 | standard-observable surrogates match calls but not edit responses; this is an observable-pool effect (MARS / GBDT on the same pool fail the same way), not a property of symbolic regression; the program has no stable, direction-specific lead over rank-window GBDT; A13 and recentring edits move too few calls to test. Conclusion: Formulas found by symbolic regression on standard jet variables reproduce the networks' decisions on ordinary jets almost as well as the if-statement program, but not how those decisions change when the jet is shifted. Other fits on the same standard variables fail the same way, so what matters is which variables a surrogate is given, not how the formula is searched for. Against tree models on the same rank-window sums the program is neither consistently better nor worse | new B6s (0.6); B3 0.65 → 0.7; B4, B6t unchanged |

Plan's "pair features damp the collinear response" not supported. Conclusion: every trained tagger changes 1–5 % of its calls when its hardest particle is replaced by two exactly collinear pieces, while the IRC-safe energy-flow network changes none; splitting more particles or removing the softest one shifts calls along the particle count (toward gluon or QCD with more particles). None of the taggers is infrared safe, but the soft-particle test uses particles softer than any in the data. Open: the 1.2 % floor of ParT_kin under any change to the hardest particle | supports new claim D5 |

PFN and EFN respond at the side-effect level, so their mW use is not separated from the edit's side effects (subjet shrinkage, lower tau21). The edit does not hold subjet masses fixed (docstring wrong). Moving top pairs onto 80.4 GeV from nearby changes no network and no MLPxW, so it does not separate the readings. Conclusion: changing the W-candidate subjet-pair mass inside top jets, with total mass and τ32 held fixed, lowers the top score of ParT, ParticleNet and LorentzNet about as much as a classifier given the W-pair mass, and 2–4 times more than a classifier that sees only the edit's side effects. These three Top-Landscape taggers use the W mass inside the top, unlike JEDI-linear on hls4ml (D1). PFN and EFN respond only at the side-effect level, and the 60 GeV edits produce jets a classifier can tell from real ones | refines D1 (scope: hls4ml/JetClass); new D6 (0.7, ParT/PN/LNet) |

The hardest-particle collinear split moves them toward gluon by +0.08 to +0.21 and the ΔN = 0 control by as much; which function of the hard-particle energy sharing is used is not identified (not shown to be pT^D). Poisson overpredicts the data LR(N) slope by 4.8× because measured multiplicity is about twice as wide as Poisson. Conclusion: trained quark–gluon taggers (ParT, ParticleNet, LorentzNet) do not count particles: adding a realistic soft particle or removing the softest one changes their output by at most about 0.03 in logit, while a count-only likelihood ratio moves 0.1. Splitting the hardest particle into two collinear pieces moves them toward gluon whether or not the count changes, so they are collinear unsafe through how the hard particles share energy. Redrawn ParT_full predicts one class for 99.95 % (10-class changes 0.27–0.47 %); redrawn JEDI score moves 0.26–1.09 sd, never crosses 0 | corrected reading (H8v): IRC-safe axis-free surrogates match the networks' rankings on natural jets (ADO 0.92–0.98 except ParT Z/W 0.66–0.76) and change no calls under WTA or translation by construction, so those tests only restate that the networks are not axis invariant (D4/A4). Natural-jet ADO cannot tell m from m_A; a surrogate with m_A reproduces JEDI's WTA response (Z/W 37.7 vs 37.6 %) and most of its translation response, and gives ParT responses of the same order but larger (r 0.3–0.6). Not accounted for by m_A: the collinear-split response, JEDI's boost (ΣpT) response, ParT's Z/W ranking. Selected sets beyond the first observable vary with the sample; redrawn nulls only test binary-score sensitivity. Conclusion: stand-ins built from standard jet shapes rank W/QCD and top/QCD jets like each network in 92–97 % of pairs but never respond to the jet-axis convention, because they are axis-free by construction. Writing the jet mass as the mass about the network's given axis gives an equally good stand-in that reproduces JEDI-linear's axis response (37.7 vs 37.6 % of Z/W calls), so that response is mostly one known mass variable. The collinear-split and pT-scaling responses and ParT's Z vs W decision are not explained by either stand-in | supports D4, D4m (JEDI); new D8 (0.5); thesis note "beyond the IRC-safe literature" overstated |

For JEDI-linear 62 % of B-changed calls are W/Z → t, the D1 ΣpT shortcut; at the W/Z boundary Δ_B = Δ_P + Δ_A with P and A in opposite mass directions (the "reversed mass ordering" reading is withdrawn). EFN and ptnorm B = A follows from their pT-fraction inputs (not a finding); only ptflat bears on weakness 7. JetClass ParT/PN change 1.5–4× fewer calls under B than under P or A (full models 1.5–1.9× vs P), 2.5–9× more than R; Top ParT/PN/LorentzNet 3–4× fewer than P/A although trained in a narrow 550–650 GeV window, so "training-pT width or four-vector input" is not supported as the cause of the hls4ml-vs-Top difference. Absolute rates are not comparable across datasets. m_A changes up to 0.71 % (hls4ml) to 13 % (JetClass) per jet under B (mean ≤ 1e-4), not "≤ 0.12 %". Conclusion: boosting a jet along its own axis keeps its invariant mass (and its axis-moment mass) fixed but changes a third of JEDI-linear's calls (33 % vs 2.4 % for a matched rotation) and those of every other hls4ml-trained tagger; most of JEDI-linear's changed calls are W or Z jets becoming top, which also happens when only pT is lowered, i.e. the known ΣpT shortcut. JetClass and Top Landscape taggers change several times fewer calls under the boost than under pT-only or angle-only scaling. Why the hls4ml and Top taggers differ is not known: both were trained in narrow pT windows on equivalent particle inputs | new D9 (0.55); refines D1 (H3 boost was a first-order mass-preserving boost; exact pT-only gives W 87 → 48 %); weakness 7 only partly addressed (ptflat) |

Holm over 240: 182 p < 0.05 | corrected reading (H13v): every network loses signal efficiency faster than the soft-drop windows at ρ ≥ 10; the induced axis offset is not the cause (c3 0–2.5 %, pufix ≤ 0.5 pp). H13's "the response comes from the added soft momentum and mass" holds only for JEDI-linear (c5 reproduces its W loss, 39.0 vs 43.3 %; c4 does not, 87.5 %; per-jet κ with W(m) 0.32; 58 % of lost W calls go to q/g). JetClass ParT_full/PN_full and Top ParT, LorentzNet, PFN respond mainly to the number of added soft particles (c4 ≈ or > pufix; c5, c2k small); JetClass kin models to both; EFN to neither alone. "Top networks lose tags faster than both cut-based taggers" holds at matched working point for ρ 5–20, not at ρ 40 (ParT, EFN above top(m, τ32)). c1 removes original particles too (2.6–22 % calls by itself); H13's c2 not pT-matched on hls4ml (c2k fixes it). Conclusion: pileup-like soft particles lower every network's signal efficiency faster than a soft-drop mass tagger, and the small axis shift they cause explains almost none of it. JEDI-linear reacts to the mass they add (one particle with the same mass and pT gives W efficiency 39 % vs 43 %), whereas the particle-ID JetClass networks and the Top Landscape ParT, LorentzNet and PFN react mainly to how many soft particles are added, even at the jet centre. When only the count changes (an exact collinear split of soft particles), quark–gluon networks move by 0.1–0.3 of the count-only likelihood ratio and JetClass W-vs-QCD networks by 1.2–2.3 times it, so whether a network uses the count depends on the model and training data, not on the edit. H18's single-particle (e1) comparison does not show this: the added particle had about 0.1 GeV in QG jets and about 1 GeV in JetClass jets. Open: whether the JetClass split halves (about 0.8 GeV) lie below the soft range the JetClass networks were trained on, and why top taggers move toward QCD while the count reference moves toward top.
